Since the paths in $\mathcal{P}$ are vertex-disjoint, the vertices of $D$ used across different Type~B paths are all distinct, giving at most $\lfloor \nicefrac{\ell}{2} \rfloor$ Type~B paths.

In total, the number of active vertices from $C_w$ is at most $\lfloor \nicefrac{\ell}{2} \rfloor + 2$.
\end{proof}

        \begin{observation}[$\star$]   
       \begin{observation}   \label{obs:proj-ext-cvd} Let $H$ be obtained from $G$ as described above. Let $uv \in E(H)$. Then:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Suppose $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}^\prime$ are $uv$-compatible in $H$. Then $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}} := \ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}} \cup \{w\}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}^\prime := \ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}^\prime \cup \{w\}$ are $uv$-compatible in $G$ and $w$-good.
  \item Suppose $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}^\prime$ are $uv$-compatible in $G$ and $w$-good. Then $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}} := \ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}} - \{w\}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}^\prime := \ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}^\prime - \{w\}$ are $uv$-compatible in $H$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{observation}
       
\begin{proof}
The first part follows from the fact that we can simply execute the guard movements that witness the $uv$-compatibility of $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}^\prime$ in $H$ in $G$, since $H$ is a subgraph of $G$. None of these movements will involve the vertex $\{w\}$, since $w \notin V(H)$.


For the second part, note that $u \neq w$ and $v \neq w$ since $uv \in E(H)$.
By~\Cref{obs:active-vertex-bound}, in a minimal transition witnessing the $uv$-compatibility of $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}^\prime$, at most $\lfloor \nicefrac{\ell}{2} \rfloor + 2$ vertices from $C_w$ are active. If $w$ is not active, then this transition does not involve $w$ at all and can be carried out verbatim in $H$. Otherwise $w$ is active; since $w$ is removed only when $C$ contains more than $\ell + 1$ vertices of signature $\sigma(w)$, we have $|C_w| \geqslant \ell + 2$, and as the number of active vertices is at most $\lfloor \nicefrac{\ell}{2} \rfloor + 2 \leqslant \ell + 1 < |C_w|$, there is a non-active vertex $w' \in C_w$; moreover $w' \neq w$ because $w$ is active. We then replace $w$ with $w'$ on the witness paths: this is valid because $w$ and $w'$ have identical neighborhoods in $D$ and belong to the same clique component $C$. The resulting transition avoids $w$ entirely and can therefore be carried out in $H$.
\end{proof}
   

Define a $w$-good strategy as one in which every configuration used is $w$-good.

        \begin{observation}[$\star$]   
       \begin{observation}   \label{obs:wgood}
  If there is a winning strategy for $G$ using $k$ guards, then there is a $w$-good winning strategy using $k$ guards.
\end{observation}
       
\begin{proof}
Let $f_d$ be a winning strategy for $G$. We construct a $w$-good
strategy $f_d^*$ as follows. For any edge $e$ not incident to $w$: by
the active vertex bound obtained in~\Cref{obs:active-vertex-bound}, $w$ can remain stationary during any defense, so if the current configuration is $w$-good, the next one is too. For an edge $e = wa$ incident to $w$: if defending $e$ leaves $w$ unoccupied, then since $C$ is a clique and any vertex cover of $G$ must cover all but at most one vertex of $C$, there exists $v \in C$ with $\sigma(v) = \sigma(w)$ that is occupied. Since $v$ and $w$ have identical neighborhoods outside $C$ and are adjacent within $C$, swapping their roles in the defense yields an equivalent $w$-good configuration.
\end{proof}
   

        \begin{lemma}[$\star$]   
       \begin{lemma}    \label{thm:cvd-bdd}
$evc(G) = evc(H) + 1$.
\end{lemma}
       

                   
              
                                   
                        
                                      
                                                                      
                                                                                          
                                                                                            
                                                                                                             
                                                                                                         
                                                                                                          
                                                          
                                                         
                                                         
     
                                               
                                                  

                                                                         
                                                                                

                                                                     
                                         
                                                                     
                                         
                                                             
                                                             

                                               
                                               
                                                
                                              
                                              
                                              
                                        
                                         
                                             

                                               
                                               
                                               
                                                
                                                 
                                              
                                          
                                         
                                                

                                                          
                                                  
                                                              
                                                      

                                                 
                                                       
                                                 
                                                       

                                                                                 
                         
                                                                                 
                          
                                                                                  
                          
                                                                                   
                          

                                                   
                                                                             
                   

                                                        
                                                                 
                      
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
                                     
              

\begin{proof}

\textbf{The Forward Direction.} We show $evc(H) \leqslant evc(G) - 1$. By \Cref{obs:wgood}, let $f_d$ be a winning $w$-good strategy for $G$ using $evc(G)$ guards.

Define $f_d^\prime$ for $H$ using $evc(G) - 1$ guards as follows: for any configuration $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$ of $H$ and edge $e \in E(H)$, let $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}},e) = f_d(\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}} \cup \{w\},e) - \{w\}$. Since $f_d$ is $w$-good, $f_d(\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}} \cup \{w\},e)$ contains $w$, so the projection is well-defined and has one guard less. By the second part of \Cref{obs:proj-ext-cvd}, if $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}} \cup \{w\}$ and $f_d(\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}} \cup \{w\},e)$ are $e$-compatible in $G$, then $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$ and $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}},e)$ are $e$-compatible in $H$. It is easily verified that $f_d^\prime$ is winning, based on the fact that $f_d$ is winning.

\textbf{The Reverse Direction.} We now show that $evc(G) \leqslant evc(H) + 1$. Suppose $f_d$ is a winning strategy for $H$ that uses $evc(H)$ guards. Define $f_d^\prime$ as a partial strategy for $G$ as follows: for any $e \in E(H)$, let $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}},e) = f_d(\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}} - \{w\},e) \cup \{w\}$. Note that $f_d^\prime$ is a valid partial strategy because of~\Cref{obs:proj-ext-cvd} combined with the assumption that $e \in E(H)$. Also note that this partial strategy can defend all attacks on edges that are not incident on $w$.

Now suppose some edge $e$ incident on $w$ is attacked, say $e = wy$, when $G$ is in configuration $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$. Note that there exists a vertex $x$ with $\sigma(x) = \sigma(w)$ that is occupied in $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$, since there are $\ell + 1$ vertices in $C$ whose signature is the same as that of $w$, $\ell + 1 \geqslant 2$, and $C$ is a clique. Therefore, we defend according to $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}},xy)$, but replace $x$ with $w$. We refer to $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}},xy)$ as the original defense and our adaptation of it as the switched defense. Note that the vertex $x$ does not participate in the switched defense at all, since the roles of $x$ and $w$ are switched.

There are two scenarios in terms of how the original defense interacted with the vertex $x$. First, it is possible that the guard on $x$ was moved out of $x$ and some guard was moved into $x$. In the switched defense, $w$ ends up with a guard, and $x$ does not participate in the defense. Thus the resulting configuration is $w$-good and identical to $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}},xy)$. On the other hand, it is possible that the guard on $x$ was moved out of $x$ and no guard was moved into $x$. Thus, at the end of the original defense, $x$ is unoccupied. In this case, at the end of the switched defense, we move the guard on $x$ to $w$. This is a valid move because in the switched defense, $x$ was occupied to begin with, did not participate in the defense, and $w$ was unoccupied at the end. So after this final move, we have again ended up with a configuration that is $w$-good and identical to $f_d^\prime(\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}},xy)$. This concludes the proof.
\end{proof}
   

Based on repeated applications of \Cref{thm:cvd-bdd}, we observe that we can start with a graph $G$ and a \textsf{CVD}{} $D$ and for every signature $\sigma$, remove all but $\ell + 1$ vertices with signature $\sigma$ from any clique $C$ in $G - D$, and be left with an equivalent instance that has the same \textsf{CVD}{} as the original, but with the guard budget adjusted by the number of vertices removed. This new instance (say $H$) is such that all cliques in $H - D$ have size at most $2^\ell \cdot (\ell + 1)$. Now this instance admits a fixed-parameter tractable algorithm by a reduction to the case of parameterizing by the size of a deletion set into components of bounded size.
       
\begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{the:evc-fpt-cvd}]
Let $G$ be a graph with a \textsf{CVD}{} $D$ of size $\ell$. By \Cref{obs:connected-deletion-set-cvd}, we may assume that $G[D]$ is connected, at the cost of increasing $\ell$ by a factor of at most three. There are at most $2^\ell$ distinct signatures $\sigma \subseteq D$. For each clique $C$ in $G - D$ and each signature $\sigma$, if there are more than $\ell + 1$ vertices in $C$ with signature $\sigma$, we repeatedly apply \Cref{thm:cvd-bdd} to remove vertices one at a time until exactly $\ell + 1$ vertices with signature $\sigma$ remain in $C$. Let $w$ denote the total number of vertices removed across all cliques and all signatures. By \Cref{thm:cvd-bdd}, each removal decreases $evc$ by exactly one, so the resulting graph $H$ satisfies $evc(G) = evc(H) + w$.

After this reduction, each clique in $H - D$ has at most $2^\ell \cdot (\ell + 1)$ vertices, since there are at most $2^\ell$ possible signatures and at most $\ell + 1$ vertices per signature. Setting $p := 2^\ell \cdot (\ell + 1)$, we observe that $D$ is also a \textsf{SCD}{} of $H$ with largest component size at most $p$.

We now argue that every clique $C$ in $H - D$ is self-sufficient in
the sense of \Cref{def:self-sufficient}. Let $s = |V(C)|$.
Because $C$ is a clique any winning configuration of $G$ must contain at least $s-1$
vertices in $C$. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that every vertex $c \in V(C)$ gives rise to a winning configuration $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ of $G$ with $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}\cap V(C)=V(C)\setminus \{c\}$, i.e., consider the configuration that contains all vertices of $G$ apart from the the vertex $c$. Therefore, $\{ V(C)\setminus\{c\} \mid c \in V(C)\}$ is the set of minimum configurations of $C$, and since one can easily go between any two of such configurations in one step, it follows that $C$ is self-sufficient.

           
                                                               
                                                                      
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   

                                                                    
                   
Since all components of $H - D$ are self-sufficient and have size at most $p$, and the result now follows from~\Cref{the:evc-fpt-scd}.
\end{proof}
   

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            




\@startsection {section}{1}{\z@} 
                                   {-3.5ex \@plus -1ex \@minus -.2ex} 
                                   {2.3ex \@plus.2ex} 
                                   {\sffamily\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\Large{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}\bfseries\raggedright}{Deletion to Small Components}
\label{sec:scd}

\@startsection{subsection}{2}{\z@} 
                                     {-3.25ex\@plus -1ex \@minus -.2ex} 
                                     {1.5ex \@plus .2ex} 
                                     {\sffamily\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\@setfontsize\Large{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}{12}{14}\bfseries\raggedright}{A Lower Bound}
To develop a lower bound, we need to examine the components in isolation, but at the same time capture the dynamics of its interaction with the rest of the instance via the deletion set. To this end, we introduce an auxiliary problem that simplifies the outside interaction by empowering the deletion set directly and bringing focus to just one component.

In the {\sc Bootstrapped EVC} problem, we are given a graph $G=(V,E)$, a subset $S\subseteq V$ and a positive integer $t$.  The goal is to determine if there is there a configuration $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ of an eternal vertex cover of $G$ such that $|\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}} \cap S|\leq t$?

Note that {\sc Bootstrapped EVC} is a generalization of {\sc EVC} as the two problems coincide when $S=V$. Let $bevc(G,S)=\min \{t~|~(G,S,t)$ is a YES instance for {\sc Bootstrapped EVC}\}. Let $D\subseteq V$ be a vertex set and denote by $\mathcal{D}$ the set of components of $G-D$. Denote the size of a largest component in $\mathcal{D}$ by $p$ and the size of $D$ by $\ell$. Let $C$ be a component in $\mathcal{D}$. We define the graph $G(C)$ that is obtained from $G[D\cup C]$ after adding a set $B$ of pairwise non-adjacent new vertices of size $\ell+p+1$ such that $B$ and $D$ form the two partition classes of a complete bipartite graph. See \Cref{fig:gc} for an example of $G(C)$.

\centering

\label{fig:gc}
\end{figure}

A {\it state} $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$ for a component $C$ is a subset of vertices of $C$. We say that a component $C\in \mathcal{D}$ is {\it defendable} w.r.t. a state $\ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$, if $D\cup B\cup \ensuremath{{\color{SeaGreen}\mathbf{R}}}$ is winning in $G(C)$. Let $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'$ be a configuration of $G$ and $G(C)$, respectively; we say that $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'$ {\it dominates} $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ if $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}\cap (D\cup C)\subseteq \ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'\cap (D\cup C)$.

Let $lb(C)$ be equal to $bevc(G(C),C)$: this value lower bounds the minimum number of guards present in the component $C$ in a configuration of any eternal vertex cover of $G(C)$.
                                                                                       

        \begin{restatable}[$\star$]{lemma}{lbdbound}   
       \begin{restatable}{lemma}{lbdbound}   \label{obs:lowerbound}
Let $G=(V,E)$ be a graph, $D\subseteq V$, and let
$\mathcal{D}$ be the set of components of $G\setminus D$. Then, $$lb_D(G)=\sum_{C\in \mathcal{D}} lb(C)\leq evc(G).$$
\end{restatable}
       
\begin{proof}
Let $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}\subseteq V$ be any configuration of $G$ with $|\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}|<lb_D(G)$. Then, there exists a component $C\in \mathcal{D}$, such that the state of $C$, i.e. $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}\cap V(C)$, has size smaller than $lb(C)$. We show that $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ is losing in $G$.

Since $|\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}\cap V(C)|<lb(C)$, the configuration $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'=D\cup B\cup (\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}\cap V(C))$ is losing in $G(C)$. Therefore, there is a winning strategy $f_a'$ for the attacker on $G(C)$ starting from $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'$.

It remains to show that $f_a'$ also wins in $G$. We do so by starting from $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ and showing the contrapositive, i.e., we show that if the defender has a winning strategy in $G$ starting from $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$, then the defender has a winning strategy in $G(C)$ starting from $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'$.

                                                                                                                                                           

\newcommand{\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{P}}

                               
                                                                                                                                                    
            
              
Let $f_d$ be a winning strategy for the defender on $G$ starting from $\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}$ and define the strategy $f_d'$ for the defender on $G(C)$ as follows.

Let $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G$ be any configuration in $G$ of size $|\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}|$ and let $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$ be a configuration of $G(C)$ with $|\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}|=|\ensuremath{{\color{LightSlateBlue}\mathbf{T}}}'|$ and $D\subseteq \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$ that dominates $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G$. Now consider an attack on an edge $e$ starting from position $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$ in $G(C)$.
If $e$ is an edge in $E(G[D\cup B])$, then $f_d'(\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}},e)$ can be set to a configuration isomorphic to $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$. Therefore, we can assume that $e$ is incident to at least one vertex in $C$, which implies that $e$ is also an in $G$. Let $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G'=f_d(\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G,e)$.
 We will show next that the defender can reach a position $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}'$ from $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$ (while defending $e$) such that $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}'$ dominates $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}'_G$ and $D \subseteq \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}'$, and this means we can set $f_d'(\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}},e)=\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}'$.

Let $\mathcal{P}_G$ be the set of vertex-disjoint paths that witness that $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G'$ are $e$-compatible and whose existence is guaranteed by \Cref{obs:k-disjoint-paths}. We prove how to obtain a set of vertex-disjoint paths $\mathcal{P}$ from $\mathcal{P}_G$ that shows that $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$ and $\ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}'$ are $e$-compatible.
Let $P=(v_1,\ldots,v_t)\in \mathcal{P}_G$, for some $t\geq 2$. Without loss
of generality, we can assume that $v_1\in \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G\setminus \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G'$ and $v_t\in \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G'\setminus \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}_G$. Now we do classify $P$ depending on the location of $v_1$ and $v_t$ as follows. The path $P$ is of
\begin{itemize}
\item {\bf Type I} if $v_1\in V(C)$ and $v_t\in V(C)\setminus \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$;
\item {\bf Type II} if $v_1\in D\cup V(C')$, for some component $C'\in \mathcal{D}$ and $v_t\in V(C)\setminus \ensuremath{{\color{Orchid}\mathbf{S}}}$;
\item {\bf Type III} if $P$ is not of Type I or II.
\end{itemize}
See also \Cref{fig:types}. Moreover, let $1\leq d\leq d'\leq t$ be the minimum and maximum, respectively, integers such that $v_d$ and $v_{d'}$ belong to $D$.
